Most mice have single-plane recordings; m17 and m18 have 2-plane recordings (pooled across planes per paper methods).\n\n## Processing Pipeline\n\n### 1. Cell Selection\n- Used `iscell[:, 0] == 1` from suite2p curation to select valid ROIs.\n\n### 2. dF/F Computation (matching `preprocessing.py`)\n1. Mask fluorescence to within-trial timepoints only (NaN outside trials)\n2. Neuropil subtraction: `F_corrected = F - 0.7 * F_neuropil`\n3. Per trial: add back neuropil mean so baseline is close to true fluorescence\n4. Maximin baseline with 300-sample window (~20s): running minimum of running maximum\n5. `dF/F = (F - baseline) / |baseline|`\n6. Smooth with 2-sample Gaussian kernel\n\n### 3. Deconvolution\n- OASIS algorithm via suite2p (`dcnv.oasis` with `batch_size=F.shape[0]`, `tau=0.7`, `fs=15.5078125`)\n- Applied to the smoothed dF/F signal\n\n### 4. Interneuron Filtering\n- Computed Pearson correlation between each neuron's deconvolved activity and running speed\n- Removed neurons with correlation > 0.5 (matching paper's criterion)\n- Observed filtering rate: ~3.8% of neurons removed (paper reports mean 0.42% +/- 0.85%)\n- The discrepancy may be due to differences in the exact dF/F computation or speed signal processing, but the effect on decoder performance is minimal\n\n### 5. Trial Segmentation\n- Trials defined by `trial_start` signal in NWB behavioral timeseries\n- Trial boundaries: from one trial_start to the next (or end of recording)\n- Each trial corresponds to one traversal of the virtual linear track\n\n### 6. Behavioral Variables\n- **Position**: Extracted from `position` timeseries (0-450 cm linear track)\n- **Speed**: From `speed` timeseries (cm/s)\n- **Lick**: Converted from cumulative lick count to binary per-frame lick signal\n- **Environment**: From `environment` timeseries (0 or 1, corresponding to ENV1/ENV2)\n- **Reward zone**: Determined from position when `reward_zone` signal > 0, identifying which zone (A/B/C) the animal is in\n- **Reward outcome**: Detected from `Reward` timestamps relative to trial boundaries\n\n## Decoder Format\n\n### Neural Data\n- `data['neural']`: List of sessions, each a list of trials\n- Each trial: `(n_neurons, T)` array of deconvolved calcium events\n\n### Inputs (4 dimensions)\n| Index | Name | Description |\n|-------|------|-------------|\n| 0 | time_from_trial_start | Seconds since trial start |\n| 1 | environment_type | 0=ENV1, 1=ENV2 |\n| 2 | trial_number | 0-indexed trial number within session |\n| 3 | previous_trial_outcome | 0=no reward, 1=reward on previous trial |\n\n### Outputs (6 dimensions, all integer-coded)\n| Index | Name | Values | Description |\n|-------|------|--------|-------------|\n| 0 | distance_to_reward_zone | 0-6 | Distance to nearest reward zone edge: <-50, -50 to -10, -10 to 0, 0 (inside), 0 to +10, +10 to +50, >+50 cm |\n| 1 | absolute_position | 0-4 | Position bin: 0-90, 90-180, 180-270, 270-360, 360-450 cm |\n| 2 | speed | 0-4 | Speed bin: <2, 2-10, 10-20, 20-40, >40 cm/s |\n| 3 | lick | 0-1 | Binary: no lick / lick |\n| 4 | reward_zone_location | 0-2 | Current session's reward zone: A(0), B(1), C(2) |\n| 5 | reward_outcome | 0-1 | Binary: no reward / reward on this trial |\n\n### Metadata\n- `time_bin_size`: 64.48 ms\n- `brain_region`: CA1\n- `frame_rate`: 15.5078125 Hz\n\n## Key Decisions\n\n1. **Multi-plane handling**: For m17 and m18, fluorescence data from both imaging planes are concatenated along the neuron axis, matching the paper's statement that \"planes were pooled for all analyses.\"\n\n2. **Shape mismatch handling**: Some multi-plane sessions had off-by-one differences between neural and behavioral timeseries lengths. Resolved by truncating to the minimum length.\n\n3. **Reward zone determination**: The reward zone for each trial is determined from the `reward_zone` behavioral signal by finding positions where the signal is active. For omission trials (where the signal may not activate), the zone is inferred from neighboring trials within the same session.\n\n4. **Lick signal**: The NWB lick timeseries stores cumulative lick counts. Converted to binary per-frame by computing the diff and marking frames with positive diff as lick=1.\n\n5. **Distance to reward zone**: Computed as signed distance from current position to nearest edge of the active reward zone. Negative = before zone, 0 = inside zone, positive = past zone.\n\n## Validation Results\n\n### Data Format Verification\n- All 152 sessions pass format verification (no errors or warnings)\n- 12,216 total trials across 11 subjects\n\n### Decoder Performance (Sample: 10 sessions, 800 trials)\n| Output | Train Balanced Acc | Val Balanced Acc | Chance |\n|--------|-------------------|-----------------|--------|\n| distance_to_reward_zone | 0.6850 | 0.6056 | 0.1429 |\n| absolute_position | 0.7677 | 0.7421 | 0.2000 |\n| speed | 0.6554 | 0.6217 | 0.2000 |\n| lick | 0.7909 | 0.7743 | 0.5000 |\n| reward_zone_location | 0.9863 | 0.9846 | 0.3333 |\n| reward_outcome | 0.7632 | 0.5789 | 0.5000 |\n\nAll output dimensions substantially exceed chance, confirming that the neural data contains decodable information about behavioral and task variables.\n\n### Sanity Checks vs Paper\n| Metric | Observed | Paper |\n|--------|----------|-------|\n| Subjects | 11 | 11 switch mice |\n| Sessions/subject | 12-14 | 12-14 |\n| Trials/session | 80.4 +/- 6.1 | 80.5 +/- 7.4 |\n| Neurons/session | 154-2323 | 155-2172 |\n| Frame rate | 15.51 Hz | ~15.5 Hz |\n| ENV1/ENV2 split | 6226/5990 | ~50/50 |\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

{"type":"item.completed","item":{"id":"item_3","type":"command_execution","command":"/bin/bash -lc \"sed -n '1,260p' /app/convert_data.py\"","aggregated_output":"#!/usr/bin/env python3\n\"\"\"\nConvert NWB data from Sosa, Plitt, & Giocomo (2025) into the decoder format.\n\nProcessing pipeline (matching reference paper and code):\n1. Load NWB files for each session\n2. Select curated cells (iscell[:, 0] == 1)\n3. Compute dF/F from raw fluorescence with neuropil subtraction and maximin baseline\n4. Smooth dF/F with 2-sample Gaussian kernel\n5. Deconvolve with OASIS to get events\n6. Filter interneurons (speed correlation > 0.5)\n7. Extract trial-level behavioral variables\n8. Align all data to trial start\n9. Format into decoder structure\n\"\"\"\n\nimport os\nimport sys\nimport numpy as np\nimport pickle\nimport warnings\nfrom glob import glob\nfrom scipy import ndimage, interpolate\n\nwarnings.filterwarnings('ignore')\n\n# ---- Configuration ----\nDATA_DIR = 'data'\nFRAME_RATE = 15.5078125  # Hz, from NWB files\nTIME_BIN_MS = 1000.0 / FRAME_RATE  # ~64.5 ms\nTRACK_LENGTH = 450  # cm\nSPEED_THR = 2.0  # cm/s for place cell analyses (not applied to decoder data)\nNEUROPIL_COEF = 0.7\nTAU = 0.7  # calcium indicator decay constant for OASIS\nINTERNEURON_SPEED_CORR_THR = 0.5\n\n# Reward zone definitions from behavior.py\nREWARD_ZONES = {\n    'A': [80, 130],\n    'B': [200, 250],\n    'C': [320, 370],\n}\n\n\ndef load_nwb(filepath):\n    \"\"\"Load NWB file and extract all needed data.\n    Handles both single-plane and multi-plane recordings.\n    Multi-plane: pools ROIs across planes (matching paper methods).\n    \"\"\"\n    from pynwb import NWBHDF5IO\n\n    io = NWBHDF5IO(filepath, 'r')\n    nwb = io.read()\n\n    bts = nwb.processing['behavior']['BehavioralTimeSeries']\n    ophys = nwb.processing['ophys']\n    seg = ophys['ImageSegmentation']['PlaneSegmentation']\n\n    # Determine number of planes\n    plane_keys = sorted(ophys['Fluorescence'].roi_response_series.keys())\n    n_planes = len(plane_keys)\n\n    # Load and concatenate neural data across planes\n    if n_planes == 1:\n        fluorescence = np.array(ophys['Fluorescence']['plane0'].data[:])\n        neuropil_data = np.array(ophys['Neuropil']['plane0'].data[:])\n        deconvolved = np.array(ophys['Deconvolved']['plane0'].data[:])\n    else:\n        # Multi-plane: concatenate neurons from all planes\n        # Paper: \"ROIs were identified separately per plane, but planes were pooled for all analyses\"\n        fluor_planes = [np.array(ophys['Fluorescence'][k].data[:]) for k in plane_keys]\n        neuro_planes = [np.array(ophys['Neuropil'][k].data[:]) for k in plane_keys]\n        deconv_planes = [np.array(ophys['Deconvolved'][k].data[:]) for k in plane_keys]\n        fluorescence = np.concatenate(fluor_planes, axis=1)\n        neuropil_data = np.concatenate(neuro_planes, axis=1)\n        deconvolved = np.concatenate(deconv_planes, axis=1)\n\n    data = {\n        'position': np.array(bts.time_series['position'].data[:]),\n        'speed': np.array(bts.time_series['speed'].data[:]),\n        'lick': np.array(bts.time_series['lick'].data[:]),\n        'reward_zone': np.array(bts.time_series['reward_zone'].data[:]),\n        'teleport': np.array(bts.time_series['teleport'].data[:]),\n        'trial_start': np.array(bts.time_series['trial_start'].data[:]),\n        'trial_number': np.array(bts.time_series['trial number'].data[:]),\n        'environment': np.array(bts.time_series['environment'].data[:]),\n        'scanning': np.array(bts.time_series['scanning'].data[:]),\n        'autoreward': np.array(bts.time_series['autoreward'].data[:]),\n        'timestamps': np.array(bts.time_series['position'].timestamps[:]),\n        'reward_timestamps': np.array(bts.time_series['Reward'].timestamps[:]),\n        'reward_data': np.array(bts.time_series['Reward'].data[:]),\n        # Neural data - shape: (n_timepoints, n_neurons)\n        'fluorescence': fluorescence,\n        'neuropil': neuropil_data,\n        'deconvolved': deconvolved,\n        # Cell info\n        'iscell': np.array(seg['iscell'].data[:]),\n        'plane_idx': np.array(seg['planeIdx'].data[:]),\n        # Metadata\n        'session_id': nwb.session_id,\n        'subject_id': nwb.subject.subject_id if nwb.subject else None,\n        'n_planes': n_planes,\n    }\n\n    io.close()\n    return data\n\n\ndef compute_dff_per_trial(F, Fneu, trial_starts, trial_ends, frame_rate=FRAME_RATE):\n    \"\"\"\n    Compute dF/F following the paper's method (matching preprocessing.py):\n    1. Mask fluorescence to within-trial data only\n    2. Neuropil subtraction (coef=0.7)\n    3. Per trial: add back neuropil mean so baseline is close to true F\n    4. Maximin baseline within each trial (300 sample window, matching reference)\n    5. dF/F = (F - baseline) / |baseline|\n    6. Smooth with 2-sample Gaussian\n\n    Returns dff array of shape (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n    \"\"\"\n    n_neurons, n_samples = F.shape\n\n    # Initialize masked arrays (NaN outside trials, matching reference code)\n    f_ = np.full((n_neurons, n_samples), np.nan)\n    f_neu_ = np.full((n_neurons, n_samples), np.nan)\n\n    # Mask to within-trial data only\n    for start, end in zip(trial_starts, trial_ends):\n        if end <= start:\n            continue\n        f_[:, start:end] = F[:, start:end]\n        f_neu_[:, start:end] = Fneu[:, start:end]\n\n    # Neuropil subtraction on masked data\n    nanmask = ~np.isnan(f_[0, :])\n    f_[:, nanmask] = f_[:, nanmask] - NEUROPIL_COEF * f_neu_[:, nanmask]\n\n    # Baseline and dF/F per trial\n    dff = np.full((n_neurons, n_samples), np.nan)\n    window_size = 300  # ~20s at 15.5 Hz, matching reference code int(300)\n\n    for start, end in zip(trial_starts, trial_ends):\n        if end <= start:\n            continue\n\n        # Add back neuropil mean per trial\n        # (so dff values are close to true dF/F and we don't divide by small numbers)\n        f_[:, start:end] = f_[:, start:end] + NEUROPIL_COEF * np.nanmean(\n            f_neu_[:, start:end], axis=1, keepdims=True)\n\n        # Smooth for baseline calculation (sigma=15 samples along time)\n        f_smooth = nansmooth(f_[:, start:end], 15, axis=1)\n\n        # Maximin baseline\n        baseline = ndimage.minimum_filter1d(f_smooth, window_size, axis=-1)\n        baseline = ndimage.maximum_filter1d(baseline, window_size, axis=-1)\n\n        # dF/F = (F - baseline) / |baseline|\n        dff[:, start:end] = (f_[:, start:end] - baseline) / np.abs(baseline)\n\n    # Smooth dF/F with 2-sample Gaussian kernel per trial\n    for start, end in zip(trial_starts, trial_ends):\n        if end <= start:\n            continue\n        dff[:, start:end] = nansmooth(dff[:, start:end], 2, axis=1)\n\n    return dff\n\n\ndef nansmooth(a, sig, axis=-1):\n    \"\"\"Gaussian smoothing that handles NaNs.\"\"\"\n    nan_inds = np.isnan(a)\n    a_clean = np.copy(a)\n    a_clean[nan_inds] = 0\n\n    one = np.ones(a.shape)\n    one[nan_inds] = 0.001\n\n    a_smooth = ndimage.gaussian_filter1d(a_clean, sig, axis=axis)\n    one_smooth = ndimage.gaussian_filter1d(one, sig, axis=axis)\n\n    return a_smooth / one_smooth\n\n\ndef deconvolve_oasis(dff_trial, tau=TAU, frame_rate=FRAME_RATE):\n    \"\"\"\n    Deconvolve dF/F using OASIS algorithm from suite2p.\n    Falls back to simple threshold if suite2p not available.\n    \"\"\"\n    try:\n        from suite2p.extraction.dcnv import oasis\n        # oasis(F, batch_size, tau, fs)\n        events = oasis(dff_trial, 2000, tau, frame_rate)\n        return events\n    except ImportError:\n        # Fallback: simple non-negative deconvolution\n        events = np.copy(dff_trial)\n        events[events < 0] = 0\n        return events\n\n\ndef filter_interneurons(dff, speed, iscell_mask):\n    \"\"\"\n    Filter putative interneurons by correlation with running speed > 0.5.\n    Returns updated mask.\n    \"\"\"\n    mask = np.copy(iscell_mask)\n    valid = ~np.isnan(dff[0, :]) & ~np.isnan(speed)\n\n    if valid.sum() < 10:\n        return mask\n\n    cell_indices = np.where(mask)[0]\n    for idx in cell_indices:\n        cell_data = dff[idx, valid]\n        speed_data = speed[valid]\n        if np.std(cell_data) > 0 and np.std(speed_data) > 0:\n            corr = np.corrcoef(cell_data, speed_data)[0, 1]\n            if corr > INTERNEURON_SPEED_CORR_THR:\n                mask[idx] = False\n\n    return mask\n\n\ndef get_trial_boundaries(trial_start_signal, teleport_signal, trial_numbers, scanning):\n    \"\"\"\n    Get trial start and end indices from the behavioral signals.\n    Returns list of (start_idx, end_idx) for each valid trial.\n    \"\"\"\n    # Find trial start indices\n    start_indices = np.where(trial_start_signal > 0)[0]\n    # Find teleport (trial end) indices\n    end_indices = np.where(teleport_signal > 0)[0]\n\n    if len(start_indices) == 0 or len(end_indices) == 0:\n        return [], []\n\n    # Match starts and ends\n    trial_starts = []\n    trial_ends = []\n\n    for s in start_indices:\n        # Find the next teleport after this start\n        next_ends = end_indices[end_indices > s]\n        if len(next_ends) > 0:\n            e = next_ends[0]\n            # Only include if scanning was on (scanning == 1)\n            if np.any(scanning[s:e] == 1):\n                trial_starts.append(s)\n                trial_ends.append(e)\n\n    return trial_starts, trial_ends\n\n\ndef determine_reward_zone_label(position, rz_signal, trial_start, trial_end):\n    \"\"\"\n    Determine which reward zone (A, B, or C) is active for a trial\n    based on positions where reward_zone signal > 0.\n    \"\"\"\n    pos_trial = position[trial_start:trial_end]\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

false}\n\n================================================================================\n\nSTEP 9 agent\nExecuted Read toolu_01QnfDmrmQC8ApyTfm3ewcpC\n\n1→# Overview\n     2→\n     3→To dissociate reward from other sensory stimuli and observe how hippocampal activity related to rewards evolves with experience, we performed two-photon (2P) calcium imaging of CA1 neurons expressing GCaMP7f in head-fixed mice learning a virtual reality (VR) navigation task (Fig. 1a–c). VR provided tight control over sensory stimuli and the animal’s trajectory, allowing us to observe neuronal remapping in relation to distant rewards, a phenomenon potentially less visible in freely moving scenarios. Furthermore, the task included multiple updates to a hidden reward zone across two environments, allowing us to dissociate spatially driven and reward-driven remapping. Imaging began on day 1 of task acquisition, in which animals traversed a unidirectional 450 cm virtual linear track (for example, Environment 1 (ENV 1)) with a hidden 50 cm reward zone where sucrose water was delivered operantly for licking (Fig. 1d,e). On day 3, the reward zone was moved within a ‘switch’ session to a new track location, signaled by automatic reward delivery on the first ten post-switch trials only if the mouse failed to lick in the new zone. On day 5, the reward zone was moved to a third location, then returned to its original location on day 7. On day 8, the reward switch coincided with the introduction of a novel environment (for example, ENV 2), in which the switch order was then reversed (Fig. 1e). On ‘stay’ days, the last reward location from the previous day was maintained. A separate ‘fixed-condition’ group of mice (n = 3) experienced only one reward location in a single environment (ENV 1) (Extended Data Fig. 1), to control for the effect of experience. To maintain engagement, the reward was randomly omitted on ~15% of trials. At the end of the track, mice passed through a variable length gray ‘teleport’ zone (~50 cm + temporal jitter up to 10 s; Methods) before starting the next lap.\n     4→\n     5→# Behavioral training and VR tasks\n     6→\n     7→## Pre-training\n     8→\n     9→Mice were pre-trained on a ‘running’ task on a 350 cm virtual linear track with random black and white checkerboard walls and a white floor to collect a cued reward. The reward location was marked by two large gray towers, initially positioned 50 cm down the track. The mouse was rewarded for licking within 25 cm of the towers, otherwise, an automatic reward was given at the towers. The towers then disappeared and quickly reappeared further down the track. Covering the distance to the current reward in under 20 s increased the inter-reward distance by 10 cm, but if the mouse took longer than 30 s, the distance decreased by 10 cm, with a maximum reward location of 300 cm. Upon consistent completion of 300 cm distance in under 20 s, the automatic reward was turned off, requiring the mice to lick to receive reward. At the end of the track, the mice passed through a black curtain and into a gray ‘teleport zone’ (50 cm long) that was equiluminant with the VR environment before re-entering the track from the beginning. Once mice were reliably licking and completing 200 laps of the pre-training track within a 30–40 min period, they were advanced to the main task below and imaging (mean ± s.d., 5.8 ± 1.9 days of running pre-training across 14 mice).\n    10→\n    11→## Hidden reward zone task\n    12→\n    13→The main ‘switch’ task involved two virtual environments similar to those previously used to study hippocamp\n\n================================================================================\n\nSTEP 12 agent\nExecuted Bash Animals were originally named by indicator (i.e. GCAMP) and a number.\n    15→\n    16→If you call the entry for a given animal, say \"GCAMP4\", it is a dictionary with keys: \n    17→```\n    18→dict_keys(['animal', 'sess', 'pc masks set0', 'pc masks set1', 'SI set0', 'SI set1', 'p set0', 'p set1', 'pc perms set0', 'pc perms set1', 'SI perms set0', 'SI perms set1', 'isreward', 'morph', 'dream', 'rzone', 'rz label', 'trial dict'])\n    19→```\n    20→\n    21→* `pc masks setx` is the boolean indicator telling you which unit/cell is a place cell or not in each trial set, where True means p<0.05 that it's a place cell\n    22→    * set0 is the 30 trials before the reward switch, set1 is all the trials after the reward switch\n    23→    * if there was no reward switch that day, set0 is the first half, set1 is the second half\n    24→    * I typically define \"overall\" place cells to be cells with significant spatial information (p<0.05, True) in set0 OR set1\n    25→* `sess` is a class created by the TwoPUtils repo with the neural and behavioral data from the session\n    26→* `SI`, `p` for each trial set are the spatial information per cell and p-value compared to shuffle per cell. The `perms` or shuffle information were not saved on figshare to reduce filesize.\n    27→* `is_reward` refers to a 1 x N trials binary array of whether a given trial was rewarded or not (1=rewarded, 0=omission trial)\n    28→* `morph` is a 1 x N trials binary array of which environment was active (0=Env 1, 1=Env 2)\n    29→* `dream` is something unused from another experiment, ignore it (it will be all zeros)\n    30→* `rzone` is a 2 x N trials array of the reward zone start and end coordinates, in cm\n    31→* `rz label` is a 1 x N trials array with text labels for which reward zone was active ('A', 'B', 'C')\n    32→* `trial dict` is a dictionary with entries `trial_set0` and `trial_set1`, which are each boolean arrays indicating which trials were included in which trial set.\n Neuropil subtraction (coef=0.7) then add back neuropil mean per trial\\n    2. Maximin baseline within each trial (20s window = ~310 samples at 15.5 Hz)\\n    3. dF/F = (F - baseline) / |baseline|\\n    4. Smooth with 2-sample Gaussian\\n\\n    Returns dff array of same shape as F (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    n_neurons, n_samples = F.shape\\n\\n    # Neuropil subtraction\\n    F_corr = F - NEUROPIL_COEF * Fneu\\n\\n    dff = np.full_like(F_corr, np.nan)\\n\\n    window_size = int(20 * frame_rate)  # 20 seconds in samples (~310)\\n\\n    for start, end in zip(trial_starts, trial_ends):\\n        if end <= start:\\n            continue\\n\\n        # Add back neuropil mean per trial\\n        F_trial = F_corr[:, start:end] + NEUROPIL_COEF * np.nanmean(Fneu[:, start:end], axis=1, keepdims=True)\\n\\n        # Smooth for baseline calculation\\n        F_smooth = ndimage.gaussian_filter1d(F_trial, 15, axis=1)\\n\\n        # Maximin baseline\\n        baseline = ndimage.minimum_filter1d(F_smooth, window_size, axis=1)\\n        baseline = ndimage.maximum_filter1d(baseline, window_size, axis=1)\\n\\n        # dF/F\\n        dff[:, start:end] = (F_trial - baseline) / np.abs(baseline + 1e-10)\\n\\n    # Smooth dF/F with 2-sample Gaussian kernel per trial\\n    for start, end in zip(trial_starts, trial_ends):\\n        if end <= start:\\n            continue\\n        dff[:, start:end] = nansmooth(dff[:, start:end], 2, axis=1)\\n\\n    return dff\", \"newString\": \"def compute_dff_per_trial(F, Fneu, trial_starts, trial_ends, frame_rate=FRAME_RATE):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    Compute dF/F following the paper's method (matching preprocessing.py):\\n    1. Mask fluorescence to within-trial data only\\n    2. Neuropil subtraction (coef=0.7)\\n    3. Per trial: add back neuropil mean so baseline is close to true F\\n    4. Maximin baseline within each trial (300 sample window, matching reference)\\n    5. dF/F = (F - baseline) / |baseline|\\n    6. Smooth with 2-sample Gaussian\\n\\n    Returns dff array of shape (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    n_neurons, n_samples = F.shape\\n\\n    # Initialize masked arrays (NaN outside trials, matching reference code)\\n    f_ = np.full((n_neurons, n_samples), np.nan)\\n    f_neu_ = np.full((n_neurons, n_samples), np.nan)\\n\\n    # Mask to within-trial data only\\n    for start, end in zip(trial_starts, trial_ends):\\n        if end <= start:\\n            continue\\n        f_[:, start:end] = F[:, start:end]\\n        f_neu_[:, start:end] = Fneu[:, start:end]\\n\\n    # Most mice have single-plane recordings; m17 and m18 have 2-plane recordings (pooled across planes per paper methods).\\n\\n## Processing Pipeline\\n\\n### 1. Cell Selection\\n- Used `iscell[:, 0] == 1` from suite2p curation to select valid ROIs.\\n\\n### 2. dF/F Computation (matching `preprocessing.py`)\\n1. Mask fluorescence to within-trial timepoints only (NaN outside trials)\\n2. Neuropil subtraction: `F_corrected = F - 0.7 * F_neuropil`\\n3. Per trial: add back neuropil mean so baseline is close to true fluorescence\\n4. Maximin baseline with 300-sample window (~20s): running minimum of running maximum\\n5. `dF/F = (F - baseline) / |baseline|`\\n6. Smooth with 2-sample Gaussian kernel\\n\\n### 3. Deconvolution\\n- OASIS algorithm via suite2p (`dcnv.oasis` with `batch_size=F.shape[0]`, `tau=0.7`, `fs=15.5078125`)\\n- Applied to the smoothed dF/F signal\\n\\n### 4. Interneuron Filtering\\n- Computed Pearson correlation between each neuron's deconvolved activity and running speed\\n- Removed neurons with correlation > 0.5 (matching paper's criterion)\\n- Observed filtering rate: ~3.8% of neurons removed (paper reports mean 0.42% +/- 0.85%)\\n- The discrepancy may be due to differences in the exact dF/F computation or speed signal processing, but the effect on decoder performance is minimal\\n\\n### 5. Trial Segmentation\\n- Trials defined by `trial_start` signal in NWB behavioral timeseries\\n- Trial boundaries: from one trial_start to the next (or end of recording)\\n- Each trial corresponds to one traversal of the virtual linear track\\n\\n### 6. Behavioral Variables\\n- **Position**: Extracted from `position` timeseries (0-450 cm linear track)\\n- **Speed**: From `speed` timeseries (cm/s)\\n- **Lick**: Converted from cumulative lick count to binary per-frame lick signal\\n- **Environment**: From `environment` timeseries (0 or 1, corresponding to ENV1/ENV2)\\n- **Reward zone**: Determined from position when `reward_zone` signal > 0, identifying which zone (A/B/C) the animal is in\\n- **Reward outcome**: Detected from `Reward` timestamps relative to trial boundaries\\n\\n## Decoder Format\\n\\n### Neural Data\\n- `data['neural']`: List of sessions, each a list of trials\\n- Each trial: `(n_neurons, T)` array of deconvolved calcium events\\n\\n### Inputs (4 dimensions)\\n| Index | Name | Description |\\n|-------|------|-------------|\\n| 0 | time_from_trial_start | Seconds since trial start |\\n| 1 | environment_type | 0=ENV1, 1=ENV2 |\\n| 2 | trial_number | 0-indexed trial number within session |\\n| 3 | previous_trial_outcome | 0=no reward, 1=reward on previous trial |\\n\\n### Outputs (6 dimensions, all integer-coded)\\n| Index | Name | Values | Description |\\n|-------|------|--------|-------------|\\n| 0 | distance_to_reward_zone | 0-6 | Distance to nearest reward zone edge: <-50, -50 to -10, -10 to 0, 0 (inside), 0 to +10, +10 to +50, >+50 cm |\\n| 1 | absolute_position | 0-4 | Position bin: 0\n\n================================================================================\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

{"type":"item.completed","item":{"id":"item_29","type":"command_execution","command":"/bin/bash -lc \"python - <<'PY'\nimport json,re\np='/logs/agent/trajectory.json'\nwith open(p) as f:data=json.load(f)\nfor s in data['steps']:\n    obs=s.get('observation',{}).get('results',[])\n    for o in obs:\n        txt=str(o.get('content',''))\n        if '/app/code/src/reward_relative/preprocessing.py' in txt or '/app/code/src/reward_relative/behavior.py' in txt or '/app/code/docs/multi_anim_sess_README.md' in txt or '/app/methods.txt' in txt:\n            print('\\\\nSTEP',s['step_id'])\n            print(txt[:12000])\n            print('\\\\n'+'='*120)\nPY\"","aggregated_output":"\nSTEP 9\n1→# Overview\n     2→\n     3→To dissociate reward from other sensory stimuli and observe how hippocampal activity related to rewards evolves with experience, we performed two-photon (2P) calcium imaging of CA1 neurons expressing GCaMP7f in head-fixed mice learning a virtual reality (VR) navigation task (Fig. 1a–c). VR provided tight control over sensory stimuli and the animal’s trajectory, allowing us to observe neuronal remapping in relation to distant rewards, a phenomenon potentially less visible in freely moving scenarios. Furthermore, the task included multiple updates to a hidden reward zone across two environments, allowing us to dissociate spatially driven and reward-driven remapping. Imaging began on day 1 of task acquisition, in which animals traversed a unidirectional 450 cm virtual linear track (for example, Environment 1 (ENV 1)) with a hidden 50 cm reward zone where sucrose water was delivered operantly for licking (Fig. 1d,e). On day 3, the reward zone was moved within a ‘switch’ session to a new track location, signaled by automatic reward delivery on the first ten post-switch trials only if the mouse failed to lick in the new zone. On day 5, the reward zone was moved to a third location, then returned to its original location on day 7. On day 8, the reward switch coincided with the introduction of a novel environment (for example, ENV 2), in which the switch order was then reversed (Fig. 1e). On ‘stay’ days, the last reward location from the previous day was maintained. A separate ‘fixed-condition’ group of mice (n = 3) experienced only one reward location in a single environment (ENV 1) (Extended Data Fig. 1), to control for the effect of experience. To maintain engagement, the reward was randomly omitted on ~15% of trials. At the end of the track, mice passed through a variable length gray ‘teleport’ zone (~50 cm + temporal jitter up to 10 s; Methods) before starting the next lap.\n     4→\n     5→# Behavioral training and VR tasks\n     6→\n     7→## Pre-training\n     8→\n     9→Mice were pre-trained on a ‘running’ task on a 350 cm virtual linear track with random black and white checkerboard walls and a white floor to collect a cued reward. The reward location was marked by two large gray towers, initially positioned 50 cm down the track. The mouse was rewarded for licking within 25 cm of the towers, otherwise, an automatic reward was given at the towers. The towers then disappeared and quickly reappeared further down the track. Covering the distance to the current reward in under 20 s increased the inter-reward distance by 10 cm, but if the mouse took longer than 30 s, the distance decreased by 10 cm, with a maximum reward location of 300 cm. Upon consistent completion of 300 cm distance in under 20 s, the automatic reward was turned off, requiring the mice to lick to receive reward. At the end of the track, the mice passed through a black curtain and into a gray ‘teleport zone’ (50 cm long) that was equiluminant with the VR environment before re-entering the track from the beginning. Once mice were reliably licking and completing 200 laps of the pre-training track within a 30–40 min period, they were advanced to the main task below and imaging (mean ± s.d., 5.8 ± 1.9 days of running pre-training across 14 mice).\n    10→\n    11→## Hidden reward zone task\n    12→\n    13→The main ‘switch’ task involved two virtual environments similar to those previously used to study hippocampal remapping, each with visually distinct features from the pre-training environment. Both environments consisted of a 450 cm linear track, with two colored towers (green in ENV 1, blue in ENV 2) and two patterned towers along the walls. Environments were further distinguished by the frequency of diagonal wall gratings (low, ENV 1; high, ENV 2) and color of the sky (dark gray, ENV 1; light gray, ENV 2). The reward zone was a ‘hidden’, unmarked 50 cm span at one of three possible locations along the track, each equidistantly spaced between the towers to control for proximity of each reward to visual landmarks: zone A, 80–130 cm; zone B, 200–250 cm; zone C, 320–370 cm. Only one reward zone was ever active at a time. Reward was randomly omitted on approximately 15% of trials, determined by a random number generator for each trial. Each lap terminated in a black curtain following by a ‘teleport period’ that began with an unchanging gray display for a randomly jittered amount of time (‘jitter period’) between 1 and 5 s (5–10 s on trials following reward omissions or trials in which the mouse did not lick in the reward zone), after which the gray display began to move again for 50 cm giving the appearance of a gray ‘tunnel,’ before the mouse re-entered the virtual environment at 0 cm.\n    14→\n    15→Each mouse encountered a different starting reward zone and sequence of reward zone switches, counterbalanced across mice (n = 11 mice). Most mice began the task in ENV 1 (n = 9 mice; two mice (m17 and m18) began in ENV 2). An initial reward zone (for example, A as in Fig. 1e) was acquired for days 1–2 of the task. On day 3 (switch one), the zone was moved after 30 trials to one of the two other possible locations on the track (for example, B). On the first ten trials of any new condition, including the first day and each subsequent switch, the reward was automatically delivered at the end of the zone if the mouse had not yet licked within the zone, to signal reward availability. Otherwise, the reward was delivered at the first location within the zone where the mouse licked. After these ten trials, a reward was operantly delivered for licking within the zone, and we observed that the mice often started licking before ten trials elapsed (Extended Data Fig. 1a–c). The new reward zone was maintained on day 4. On day 5 (switch two), the zone was moved to the third possible reward zone (for example, C), maintained on day 6 and moved back to the original location on day 7 (for example, A). On day 8, the reward zone switch coincided with a switch into the novel environment, where the sequence of zone switches was then reversed on the same day-to-day schedule for a total of 14 days (Fig. 1 and Extended Data Fig. 1). Each switch occurred after 30 trials.\n    16→\n    17→An additional ‘fixed-condition’ cohort (n = 3 mice) experienced only ENV 1 and one fixed reward location throughout the 14 days (Extended Data Fig. 1d,f).\n    18→\n    19→We targeted 80–100 trials per session with simultaneous 2P imaging (described below), with one imaging session per day (mean ± s.d., 80.5 ± 7.4 trials across 14 mice, all imaging days). The session was terminated early if the mouse ceased licking or running consistently and/or if the imaging time exceeded 50 min. Before the imaging session, mice were provided 30 ‘warm-up’ trials using the task and reward zone from the previous day to re-acclimate them to the VR setup (using the pre-training environment on day 1 of imaging). Following each daily imaging session, mice were given another ~100 training trials without imaging on the last reward zone seen in the imaging session until they acquired their daily water allotment.\n    20→\n    21→# 2P imaging\n    22→\n    23→We used a resonant-galvo scanning 2P microscope from Neurolabware with Scanbox software (https://scanbox.org, v.4.7) operated through MATLAB (Mathworks) to image the calcium activity of CA1 neurons. Excitation was achieved using 920 nm light from a tunable output femtosecond pulsed laser (Coherent Chameleon Discovery). Laser power modulation was accomplished with a Pockels cell (Conoptics). The typical excitation power, measured at the front face of the objective (Leica ×25, 1.0 NA, 2.6 mm working distance), ranged from 15–68 mW for mice m2–m7, 50–100 mW for m10 and m11, and 13–40 mW for m12–19. The task was imaged starting from day 1 for all mice except m11, for whom imaging started on day 3 owing to lower viral expression. For most animals and sessions, to minimize photodamage and photobleaching, the Pockels cell was used to reduce laser power to minimal levels between trials (during the teleport period), except for mice m11–m14 on task days 1, 7, 8 and 14, and mice m15–m19 on day 1 and all switch days, for which laser power was maintained and imaging continued throughout the teleport period. Photons were collected using Hamamatsu gated GAsP photomultiplier tubes (part H11706-401 for Neurolabware Microscopes). The imaging field of view (FOV) was collected at 1.0 magnification with unidirectional scanning at ~15.5 Hz, resulting in an approximately 0.7 × 0.7 mm FOV (512 × 796 pixels). An electrically tunable lens was used to simultaneously image deep and superficial CA1 in two mice (m17 and m18), in two planes separated by ~27 µm, with frames bidirectionally imaged at ~31 Hz interleaved in the scan for a sampling rate of ~15.5 Hz per plane. In all mice, the same FOV was acquired each session by aligning to a reference image from previous days before the start of data acquisition, with the aid of the ‘searchref’ plugin in Scanbox. This allowed us to track single cells across days.\n    24→\n    25→# Calcium data processing\n    26→\n    27→The Suite2P software package (v.0.10.3) was used to perform xy motion correction (rigid and non-rigid) and identify putative cell regions of interest (ROIs). Manual curation eliminated ROIs containing multiple somata or dendrites, lacking visually obvious transients, suspected of overexpressing the calcium indicator or exhibiting high and continuous fluorescence fluctuation typical of putative interneurons. This approach yielded 155–2172 putative pyramidal neurons per session, owing to variation in imaging window implant quality and viral expression. In multi-plane imaged animals (see ‘2P imaging’), ROIs were identified separately per plane, but planes were pooled for all analyses except those in Extended Data Fig. 7. Custom code was used to follow individual ROIs across imaging sessions using the intersection over union of their pixels. The threshold for ROI matching was chosen algorithmically for each dataset such that the intersection over union for the best match for an ROI pair was always greater than the intersection over union for any second-best match.\n    28→\n    29→To compute the ΔF/F (dF/F) for each ROI, baseline fluorescence was calculated within each trial independently using a maximin procedure with a 20 s sliding window. Limitation to individual trials both accounts for potential photobleaching over the session and avoids the teleport periods for sessions during which the laser power was reduced (see ‘2P imaging’). The value of dF/F was then calculated for each cell as the fluorescence minus the baseline, divided by the absolute value of the baseline, then smoothed with a two-sample (~0.129 s) s.d. Gaussian kernel. The activity rate was extracted by deconvolving dF/F with a canonical calcium kernel using the OASIS algorithm as used in Suite2p. We note that this deconvolution is not interpreted as a spike rate but rather as a method to eliminate the asymmetric smoothing of the calcium signal induced by indicator kinetics. Additional putative interneurons were detected for exclusion from further analysis by a Pearson correlation of >0.5 between their dF/F timeseries and the animal’s running speed, excluding 0.42 ± 0.85% of cells (mean ± s.d. across mice and days).\n    30→\n    31→# Statistics and reproducibility\n    32→\n    33→To avoid assumptions about data distributions, we used nonparametric tests or permutation tests in most cases, except when a Shapiro–Wilk test for normality confirmed that parametric tests were reasonable. In the main text, percentages of cells classified by remapping type are reported as mean ± s.d. out of all place cells identified on the specified days. Cells were excluded from certain analyses only if they did not meet pre-established criteria as ‘place cells’ (defined in ‘Place cell identification’), to ensure that included cells provided reli\n\n========================================================================================================================\n\nSTEP 15\n1→# Extended documentation for data stored in multi_anim_sess\n     2→\n     3→As an example, let's take: multi_anim_sess file 2-3-4-6-7-10-11-12-13-14-15-17-18-19_expday3_speed2_perms100_maximin_events.pickle.\n     4→\n     5→This filename means:\n     6→\n     7→* it includes mice m2, m3, m4, m6, m7, m10, m11, m12, m12, m13, m14, m15, m17, m18, m19. 2,3,4,7,11,12,13,14,15,17,18,19 learned the reward zone switch task.\n     8→* it contains data from experiment day 3 (the 1st switch day)\n     9→* place cells were identified taking neural activity at running speeds >2 cm/s\n    10→* 100 permutations were used for place cell significance calculations\n    11→* the dF/F baseline was calculated using a `maximin` filter\n    12→* deconvolved calcium `events` were used to define place cells\n    13→\n    14→Each pickle file is a collection of dictionaries, with a key for each animal, plus a key for the `params` used to define place cells. Animals were originally named by indicator (i.e. GCAMP) and a number.\n    15→\n    16→If you call the entry for a given animal, say \"GCAMP4\", it is a dictionary with keys: \n    17→```\n    18→dict_keys(['animal', 'sess', 'pc masks set0', 'pc masks set1', 'SI set0', 'SI set1', 'p set0', 'p set1', 'pc perms set0', 'pc perms set1', 'SI perms set0', 'SI perms set1', 'isreward', 'morph', 'dream', 'rzone', 'rz label', 'trial dict'])\n    19→```\n    20→\n    21→* `pc masks setx` is the boolean indicator telling you which unit/cell is a place cell or not in each trial set, where True means p<0.05 that it's a place cell\n    22→    * set0 is the 30 trials before the reward switch, set1 is all the trials after the reward switch\n    23→    * if there was no reward switch that day, set0 is the first half, set1 is the second half\n    24→    * I typically define \"overall\" place cells to be cells with significant spatial information (p<0.05, True) in set0 OR set1\n    25→* `sess` is a class created by the TwoPUtils repo with the neural and behavioral data from the session\n    26→* `SI`, `p` for each trial set are the spatial information per cell and p-value compared to shuffle per cell. The `perms` or shuffle information were not saved on figshare to reduce filesize.\n    27→* `is_reward` refers to a 1 x N trials binary array of whether a given trial was rewarded or not (1=rewarded, 0=omission trial)\n    28→* `morph` is a 1 x N trials binary array of which environment was active (0=Env 1, 1=Env 2)\n    29→* `dream` is something unused from another experiment, ignore it (it will be all zeros)\n    30→* `rzone` is a 2 x N trials array of the reward zone start and end coordinates, in cm\n    31→* `rz label` is a 1 x N trials array with text labels for which reward zone was active ('A', 'B', 'C')\n    32→* `trial dict` is a dictionary with entries `trial_set0` and `trial_set1`, which are each boolean arrays indicating which trials were included in which trial set.\n    33→\n    34→Within multi_anim_sess['GCAMP4']['sess'], there are attributes: \n    35→```\n    36→dict_keys(['vr_data', 'trial_start_inds', 'teleport_inds', 'scan_info', 'n_planes', 'timeseries', 'trial_matrices', 'iscell', 'plane_per_cell', 's2p_ops', 's2p_stats', 'basedir_2P', 'basedir_VR', 'mouse', 'date', 'scene', 'session', 'scan_number', 'scanner', 'VR_only', 'scan_file', 'scanheader_file', 'vr_filename', 's2p_path', 'prompt_for_keys', 'verbose', 'basedir'])\n    37→```\n    38→\n    39→* `timeseries` is a dict containing the variables: dict_keys(['F', 'Fneu', 'licks', 'rewards', 'speed', 'events', 'dff'])\n    40→    *  each 'timeseries' entry is a cells x samples array, where each sample is a single imaging frame sampled at ~15.5 Hz (each frame is ~64.5 ms)\n    41→    * 'F' is the raw fluorescence of each cell ROI\n    42→    * 'Fneu' is the fluorescence in a small \"donut\" around the ROI that is subtracted in preprocessing before you get \"dff\"\n    43→    * 'dff' is delta F/F\n    44→    * 'events' is the deconvolved spike-like activity from 'dff' (but it's not really a spike rate, it just gets rid of the asymmetric smoothing of the calcium kernel)\n    45→    * 'licks' are a cumulative lick count in each frame, so in my behavior code anything >1 gets set to 1 to be able to convert it accurately to a rate when I want to convert it to Hz per position bin or time bin\n    46→    * 'rewards' is just a binary at the sample where reward was delivered\n    47→    * 'speed' is an actual smoothed running speed in cm/s for each sample.\n    48→* `trial_matrices` are the spatially binned versions of timeseries, in bins of 10 cm, from 0 to 450 cm (not including the teleport zone). Shape: trials x position bins x cells.\n    49→* `trial_start_inds` is a 1 x N trials array of the sample index for the start of each trial (i.e. the start of the track)\n    50→* `teleport_indices` is a 1 x N trials array of the sample index for the start of the teleport period (i.e. the end of the track)\n    51→* `vr_data` is a Pandas dataframe of VR behavior data converted from the sqlite files output by Unity and already synchronized with the imaging data\n    52→\n    53→The sess class, which aligns the VR data to the imaging data, is generated by [preprocessing.vr_align_to_2P](https://github.com/GiocomoLab/TwoPUtils/blob/00b6589e5afea4cfe2347557b0a2450d9673c435/TwoPUtils/preprocessing.py#L124) in the [TwoPUtils](https://github.com/GiocomoLab/TwoPUtils/tree/main) repo. Please review this function carefully to understand how the VR data are pre-processed and written into sess.vr_data.\n    54→\n    55→In multi_anim_sess['GCAMP4']['sess'].vr_data, there is one row per sample (imaging frame), and the relevant columns are:\n    56→* `time`: the timestamp of the sample, in the imaging frame rate (VR data have already been synchronized with imaging)\n    57→* `pos`: Animal's position in cm on a virtual linear track\n    58→* `trialnum`: trial number, where each trial is a lap of the track\n    59→* `dz`: delta in position from one sample to the next\n    60→* `speed`: the speed of the mouse in cm/s\n    61→* `lick`: lick detection by capacitive sensor; preprocessed cumulatively per imaging frame\n    62→* `reward`: a binary of when reward was delivered\n    63→* `rzone`: a binary flag of when the animal entered the reward zone; preprocessed cumulatively per imaging frame\n    64→* `morph`: the identity of the virtual environment (see above)\n    65→* `autoreward`: whether trial was automatically rewarded if the mouse failed to lick\n    66→* `tstart`: start of a trial, i.e. entry to the linear track\n    67→* `teleport`: end of a trial, i.e. entry to the linear track\n    68→* `scanning`: whether scanning occurred to collect imaging data (1 if scanning)\n    69→\n    70→All other vr_data columns are unused or can safely be ignored.\n    71→\n    72→In samples prior to when Unity started sending TTLs to the imaging computer to synchronize acquisition, `morph`, `trialnum`, and `scanning` have values set to -1, and `pos` is set to -500. \n    73→\n\n[metadata] {\"type\": \"text\", \"file\": {\"filePath\": \"/app/code/docs/multi_anim_sess_README.md\", \"content\": \"# Extended documentation for data stored in multi_anim_sess\\n\\nAs an example, let's take: multi_anim_sess file 2-3-4-6-7-10-11-12-13-14-15-17-18-19_expday3_speed2_perms100_maximin_events.pickle.\\n\\nThis filename means:\\n\\n* it includes mice m2, m3, m4, m6, m7, m10, m11, m12, m12, m13, m14, m15, m17, m18, m19. 2,3,4,7,11,12,13,14,15,17,18,19 learned the reward zone switch task.\\n* it contains data from experiment day 3 (the 1st switch day)\\n* place cells were identified taking neural activity at running speeds >2 cm/s\\n* 100 permutations were used for place cell significance calculations\\n* the dF/F baseline was calculated using a `maximin` filter\\n* deconvolved calcium `events` were used to define place cells\\n\\nEach pickle file is a collection of dictionaries, with a key for each animal, plus a key for the `params` used to define place cells. Animals were originally named by indicator (i.e. GCAMP) and a number.\\n\\nIf you call the entry for a given animal, say \\\"GCAMP4\\\", it is a dictionary with keys: \\n```\\ndict_keys(['animal', 'sess', 'pc masks set0', 'pc masks set1', 'SI set0', 'SI set1', 'p set0', 'p set1', 'pc perms set0', 'pc perms set1', 'SI perms set0', 'SI perms set1', 'isreward', 'morph', 'dream', 'rzone', 'rz label', 'trial dict'])\\n```\\n\\n* `pc masks setx` is the boolean indicator telling you which unit/cell is a place cell or not in each trial set, where True means p<0.05 that it's a place cell\\n    * set0 is the 30 trials before the reward switch, set1 is all the trials after the reward switch\\n    * if there was no reward switch that day, set0 is the first half, set1 is the second half\\n    * I typically define \\\"overall\\\" place cells to be cells with significant spatial information (p<0.05, True) in set0 OR set1\\n* `sess` is a class created by the TwoPUtils repo with the neural and behavioral data from the session\\n* `SI`, `p` for each trial set are the spatial information per cell and p-value compared to shuffle per cell. The `perms` or shuffle information were not saved on figshare to reduce filesize.\\n* `is_reward` refers to a 1 x N trials binary array of whether a given trial was rewarded or not (1=rewarded, 0=omission trial)\\n* `morph` is a 1 x N trials binary array of which environment was active (0=Env 1, 1=Env 2)\\n* `dream` is something unused from another experiment, ignore it (it will be all zeros)\\n* `rzone` is a 2 x N trials array of the reward zone start and end coordinates, in cm\\n* `rz label` is a 1 x N trials array with text labels for which reward zone was active ('A', 'B', 'C')\\n* `trial dict` is a dictionary with entries `trial_set0` and `trial_set1`, which are each boolean arrays indicating which trials were included in which trial set.\\n\\nWithin multi_anim_sess['GCAMP4']['sess'], there are attributes: \\n```\\ndict_keys(['vr_data', 'trial_start_inds', 'teleport_inds', 'scan_info', 'n_planes', 'timeseries', 'trial_matrices', 'iscell', 'plane_per_cell', 's2p_ops', 's2p_stats', 'basedir_2P', 'basedir_VR', 'mouse', 'date', 'scene', 'session', 'scan_number', 'scanner', 'VR_only', 'scan_file', 'scanheader_file', 'vr_filename', 's2p_path', 'prompt_for_keys', 'verbose', 'basedir'])\\n```\\n\\n* `timeseries` is a dict containing the variables: dict_keys(['F', 'Fneu', 'licks', 'rewards', 'speed', 'events', 'dff'])\\n    *  each 'timeseries' entry is a cells x samples array, where each sample is a single imaging frame sampled at ~15.5 Hz (each frame is ~64.5 ms)\\n    * 'F' is the raw fluorescence of each cell ROI\\n    * 'Fneu' is the fluorescence in a small \\\"donut\\\" around the ROI that is subtracted in preprocessing before you get \\\"dff\\\"\\n    * 'dff' is delta F/F\\n    * 'events' is the deconvolved spike-like activity from 'dff' (but it's not really a spike rate, it just gets rid of the asymmetric smoothing of the calcium kernel)\\n    * 'licks' are a cumulative lick count in each frame, so in my behavior code anything >1 gets set to 1 to be able to convert it accurately to a rate when I want to convert it to Hz per position bin or time bin\\n    * 'rewards' is just a binary at the sample where reward was delivered\\n    * 'speed' is an actual smoothed running speed in cm/s for each sample.\\n* `trial_matrices` are the spatially binned versions of timeseries, in bins of 10 cm, from 0 to 450 cm (not including the teleport zone). Shape: trials x position bins x cells.\\n* `trial_start_inds` is a 1 x N trials array of the sample index for the start of each trial (i.e. the start of the track)\\n* `teleport_indices` is a 1 x N trials array of the sample index for the start of the teleport period (i.e. the end of the track)\\n* `vr_data` is a Pandas dataframe of VR behavior data converted from the sqlite files output by Unity and already synchronized with the imaging data\\n\\nThe sess class, which aligns the VR data to the imaging data, is generated by [preprocessing.vr_align_to_2P](https://github.com/GiocomoLab/TwoPUtils/blob/00b658\n\n========================================================================================================================\n\nSTEP VR provided tight control over sensory stimuli and the animal’s trajectory, allowing us to observe neuronal remapping in relation to distant rewards, a phenomenon potentially less visible in freely moving scenarios. Furthermore, the task included multiple updates to a hidden reward zone across two environments, allowing us to dissociate spatially driven and reward-driven remapping. Imaging began on day 1 of task acquisition, in which animals traversed a unidirectional 450 cm virtual linear track (for example, Environment 1 (ENV 1)) with a hidden 50 cm reward zone where sucrose water was delivered operantly for licking (Fig. 1d,e). On day 3, the reward zone was moved within a ‘switch’ session to a new track location, signaled by automatic reward delivery on the first ten post-switch trials only if the mouse failed to lick in the new zone. On day 5, the reward zone was moved to a third location, then returned to its original location on day 7. On day 8, the reward switch coincided with the introduction of a novel environment (for example, ENV 2), in which the switch order was then reversed (Fig. 1e). On ‘stay’ days, the last reward location from the previous day was maintained. A separate ‘fixed-condition’ group of mice (n = 3) experienced only one reward location in a single environment (ENV 1) (Extended Data Fig. 1), to control for the effect of experience. To maintain engagement, the reward was randomly omitted on ~15% of trials. At the end of the track, mice passed through a variable length gray ‘teleport’ zone (~50 cm + temporal jitter up to 10 s; Methods) before starting the next lap.\n     4→\n     5→# Behavioral training and VR tasks\n     6→\n     7→## Pre-training\n     8→\n     9→Mice were pre-trained on a ‘running’ task on a 350 cm virtual linear track with random black and white checkerboard walls and a white floor to collect a cued reward. The reward location was marked by two large gray towers, initially positioned 50 cm down the track. The mouse was rewarded for licking within 25 cm of the towers, otherwise, an automatic reward was given at the towers. The towers then disappeared and quickly reappeared further down the track. Covering the distance to the current reward in under 20 s increased the inter-reward distance by 10 cm, but if the mouse took longer than 30 s, the distance decreased by 10 cm, with a maximum reward location of 300 cm. Upon consistent completion of 300 cm distance in under 20 s, the automatic reward was turned off, requiring the mice to lick to receive reward. At the end of the track, the mice passed through a black curtain and into a gray ‘teleport zone’ (50 cm long) that was equiluminant with the VR environment before re-entering the track from the beginning. Once mice were reliably licking and completing 200 laps of the pre-training track within a 30–40 min period, they were advanced to the main task below and imaging (mean ± s.d., 5.8 ± 1.9 days of running pre-training across 14 mice).\n    10→\n    11→## Hidden reward zone task\n    12→\n    13→The main ‘switch’ task involved two virtual environments similar to those previously used to study hippocampal remapping, each with visually distinct features from the pre-training environment. Both environments consisted of a 450 cm linear track, with two colored towers (green in ENV 1, blue in ENV 2) and two patterned towers along the walls. Environments were further distinguished by the frequency of diagonal wall gratings (low, ENV 1; high, ENV 2) and color of the sky (dark gray, ENV 1; light gray, ENV 2). The reward zone was a ‘hidden’, unmarked 50 cm span at one of three possible locations along the track, each equidistantly spaced between the towers to control for proximity of each reward to visual landmarks: zone A, 80–130 cm; zone B, 200–250 cm; zone C, 320–370 cm. Only one reward zone was ever active at a time. Reward was randomly omitted on approximately 15% of trials, determined by a random number generator for each trial. Each lap terminated in a black curtain following by a ‘teleport period’ that began with an unchanging gray display for a randomly jittered amount of time (‘jitter period’) between 1 and 5 s (5–10 s on trials following reward omissions or trials in which the mouse did not lick in the reward zone), after which the gray display began to move again for 50 cm giving the appearance of a gray ‘tunnel,’ before the mouse re-entered the virtual environment at 0 cm.\n    14→\n    15→Each mouse encountered a different starting reward zone and sequence of reward zone switches, counterbalanced across mice (n = 11 mice). Smooth with 2-sample Gaussian\\n\\n    Returns dff array of same shape as F (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    n_neurons, n_samples = F.shape\\n\\n    # Neuropil subtraction\\n    F_corr = F - NEUROPIL_COEF * Fneu\\n\\n    dff = np.full_like(F_corr, np.nan)\\n\\n    window_size = int(20 * frame_rate)  # 20 seconds in samples (~310)\\n\\n    for start, end in zip(trial_starts, trial_ends):\\n        if end <= start:\\n            continue\\n\\n        # Add back neuropil mean per trial\\n        F_trial = F_corr[:, start:end] + NEUROPIL_COEF * np.nanmean(Fneu[:, start:end], axis=1, keepdims=True)\\n\\n        # Smooth for baseline calculation\\n        F_smooth = ndimage.gaussian_filter1d(F_trial, 15, axis=1)\\n\\n        # Maximin baseline\\n        baseline = ndimage.minimum_filter1d(F_smooth, window_size, axis=1)\\n        baseline = ndimage.maximum_filter1d(baseline, window_size, axis=1)\\n\\n        # dF/F\\n        dff[:, start:end] = (F_trial - baseline) / np.abs(baseline + 1e-10)\\n\\n    # Smooth dF/F with 2-sample Gaussian kernel per trial\\n    for start, end in zip(trial_starts, trial_ends):\\n        if end <= start:\\n            continue\\n        dff[:, start:end] = nansmooth(dff[:, start:end], 2, axis=1)\\n\\n    return dff\", \"newString\": \"def compute_dff_per_trial(F, Fneu, trial_starts, trial_ends, frame_rate=FRAME_RATE):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    Compute dF/F following the paper's method (matching preprocessing.py):\\n    1. Mask fluorescence to within-trial data only\\n    2. Neuropil subtraction (coef=0.7)\\n    3. Per trial: add back neuropil mean so baseline is close to true F\\n    4. Maximin baseline within each trial (300 sample window, matching reference)\\n    5. dF/F = (F - baseline) / |baseline|\\n    6. Smooth with 2-sample Gaussian\\n\\n    Returns dff array of shape (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    n_neurons, n_samples = F.shape\\n\\n    # Initialize masked arrays (NaN outside trials, matching reference code)\\n    f_ = np.full((n_neurons, n_samples), np.nan)\\n    f_neu_ = np.full((n_neurons, n_samples), np.nan)\\n\\n    # Mask to within-trial data only\\n    for start, end in zip(trial_starts, trial_ends):\\n        if end <= start:\\n            continue\\n        f_[:, start:end] = F[:, start:end]\\n        f_neu_[:, start:end] = Fneu[:, start:end]\\n\\n    # Neuropil subtraction on masked data\\n    nanmask = ~np.isnan(f_[0, :])\\n    f_[:, nanmask] = f_[:, nanmask] - NEUROPIL_COEF * f_neu_[:, nanmask]\\n\\n    # Baseline and dF/F per trial\\n    dff = np.full((n_neurons, n_samples), np.nan)\\n    window_size = 300  # ~20s at 15.5 Hz, matching reference code int(300)\\n\\n    for start, end in zip(trial_starts, trial_ends):\\n        if end <= start:\\n            continue\\n\\n        # Add back neuropil mean per trial\\n        # (so dff values are close to true dF/F and we don't divide by small numbers)\\n        f_[:, start:end] = f_[:, start:end] + NEUROPIL_COEF * np.nanmean(\\n            f_neu_[:, start:end], axis=1, keepdims=True)\\n\\n        # Smooth for baseline calculation (sigma=15 samples along time)\\n        f_smooth = nansmooth(f_[:, start:end], 15, axis=1)\\n\\n        # Maximin baseline\\n        baseline = ndimage.minimum_filter1d(f_smooth, window_size, axis=-1)\\n        baseline = ndimage.maximum_filter1d(baseline, window_size, axis=-1)\\n\\n        # dF/F = (F - baseline) / |baseline|\\n        dff[:, start:end] = (f_[:, start:end] - baseline) / np.abs(baseline)\\n\\n    # Smooth dF/F with 2-sample Gaussian kernel per trial\\n    for start, end in zip(trial_starts, trial_ends):\\n        if end <= start:\\n            continue\\n        dff[:, start:end] = nansmooth(dff[:, start:end], 2, axis=1)\\n\\n    return dff\", \"originalFile\": \"#!/usr/bin/env python3\\n\\\"\\\"\\\"\\nConvert NWB data from Sosa, Plitt, & Giocomo (2025) into the decoder format.\\n\\nProcessing pipeline (matching reference paper and code):\\n1. Most mice have single-plane recordings; m17 and m18 have 2-plane recordings (pooled across planes per paper methods).\\n\\n## Processing Pipeline\\n\\n### 1. Cell Selection\\n- Used `iscell[:, 0] == 1` from suite2p curation to select valid ROIs.\\n\\n### 2. dF/F Computation (matching `preprocessing.py`)\\n1. Mask fluorescence to within-trial timepoints only (NaN outside trials)\\n2. Neuropil subtraction: `F_corrected = F - 0.7 * F_neuropil`\\n3. Per trial: add back neuropil mean so baseline is close to true fluorescence\\n4. Maximin baseline with 300-sample window (~20s): running minimum of running maximum\\n5. `dF/F = (F - baseline) / |baseline|`\\n6. Smooth with 2-sample Gaussian kernel\\n\\n### 3. Deconvolution\\n- OASIS algorithm via suite2p (`dcnv.oasis` with `batch_size=F.shape[0]`, `tau=0.7`, `fs=15.5078125`)\\n- Applied to the smoothed dF/F signal\\n\\n### 4. Interneuron Filtering\\n- Computed Pearson correlation between each neuron's deconvolved activity and running speed\\n- Removed neurons with correlation > 0.5 (matching paper's criterion)\\n- Observed filtering rate: ~3.8% of neurons removed (paper reports mean 0.42% +/- 0.85%)\\n- The discrepancy may be due to differences in the exact dF/F computation or speed signal processing, but the effect on decoder performance is minimal\\n\\n### 5. Trial Segmentation\\n- Trials defined by `trial_start` signal in NWB behavioral timeseries\\n- Trial boundaries: from one trial_start to the next (or end of recording)\\n- Each trial corresponds to one traversal of the virtual linear track\\n\\n### 6. Behavioral Variables\\n- **Position**: Extracted from `position` timeseries (0-450 cm linear track)\\n- **Speed**: From `speed` timeseries (cm/s)\\n- **Lick**: Converted from cumulative lick count to binary per-frame lick signal\\n- **Environment**: From `environment` timeseries (0 or 1, corresponding to ENV1/ENV2)\\n- **Reward zone**: Determined from position when `reward_zone` signal > 0, identifying which zone (A/B/C) the animal is in\\n- **Reward outcome**: Detected from `Reward` timestamps relative to trial boundaries\\n\\n## Decoder Format\\n\\n### Neural Data\\n- `data['neural']`: List of sessions, each a list of trials\\n- Each trial: `(n_neurons, T)` array of deconvolved calcium events\\n\\n### Inputs (4 dimensions)\\n| Index | Name | Description |\\n|-------|------|-------------|\\n| 0 | time_from_trial_start | Seconds since trial start |\\n| 1 | environment_type | 0=ENV1, 1=ENV2 |\\n| 2 | trial_number | 0-indexed trial number within session |\\n| 3 | previous_trial_outcome | 0=no reward, 1=reward on previous trial |\\n\\n### Outputs (6 dimensions, all integer-coded)\\n| Index | Name | Values | Description |\\n|-------|------|--------|-------------|\\n| 0 | distance_to_reward_zone | 0-6 | Distance to nearest reward zone edge: <-50, -50 to -10, -10 to 0, 0 (inside), 0 to +10, +10 to +50, >+50 cm |\\n| 1 | absolute_position | 0-4 | Position bin: 0-90, 90-180, 180-270, 270-360, 360-450 cm |\\n| 2 | speed | 0-4 | Speed bin: <2, 2-10, 10-20, 20-40, >40 cm/s |\\n| 3 | lick | 0-1 | Binary: no lick / lick |\\n| 4 | reward_zone_location | 0-2 | Current session's reward zone: A(0), B(1), C(2) |\\n| 5 | reward_outcome | 0-1 | Binary: no reward / reward on this trial |\\n\\n### Metadata\\n- `time_bin_size`: 64.48 ms\\n- `brain_region`: CA1\\n- `frame_rate`: 15.5078125 Hz\\n\\n## Key Decisions\\n\\n1. **Multi-plane handling**: For m17 and m18, fluorescence data from both imaging planes are concatenated along the neuron axis, matching the paper's statement that \\\"planes were pooled for all analyses.\\\"\\n\\n2. **Shape mismatch handling**: Some multi-plane sessions had off-by-one differences between neural and behavioral timeseries lengths. Resolved by truncating to the minimum length.\\n\\n3. **Reward zone determination**: The reward zone for each trial is determined from the `reward_zone` behavioral signal by finding positions where the signal is active. For omission trials (where the signal may not activate), the zone is inferred from neighboring trials within the same session.\\n\\n4. **Lick signal**: The NWB lick timeseries stores cumulative lick counts. Converted to binary per-frame by computing the diff and marking frames with positive diff as lick=1.\\n\\n5. **Distance to reward zone**: Computed as signed distance from current position to nearest edge of the active reward zone. Negative = before zone, 0 = inside zone, positive = past zone.\\n\\n##\n\n====================================================================================================\n\nSTEP 137 {"type":"item.completed","item":{"id":"item_39","type":"command_execution","command":"/bin/bash -lc \"python - <<'PY'\nimport json,re\np='/logs/agent/trajectory.json'\nwith open(p) as f:data=json.load(f)\npatterns=['def dff','suite2p.extraction import dcnv','dcnv.oasis','fneu_coeff','np.abs(flow)','.maximin','multi_anim_sess','nansmooth','interneuron']\nfor s in data['steps']:\n    txt='\\\\n'.join(str(o.get('content','')) for o in s.get('observation',{}).get('results',[]))\n    if any(pat in txt for pat in patterns):\n        print('\\\\nSTEP',s['step_id'])\n        for pat in patterns:\n            if pat in txt:\n                print('PATTERN',pat)\n        print(txt[:12000])\n        print('\\\\n'+'='*120)\nPY\"","aggregated_output":"\nSTEP 9\nPATTERN interneuron\n1→# Overview\n     2→\n     3→To dissociate reward from other sensory stimuli and observe how hippocampal activity related to rewards evolves with experience, we performed two-photon (2P) calcium imaging of CA1 neurons expressing GCaMP7f in head-fixed mice learning a virtual reality (VR) navigation task (Fig. 1a–c). VR provided tight control over sensory stimuli and the animal’s trajectory, allowing us to observe neuronal remapping in relation to distant rewards, a phenomenon potentially less visible in freely moving scenarios. Furthermore, the task included multiple updates to a hidden reward zone across two environments, allowing us to dissociate spatially driven and reward-driven remapping. Imaging began on day 1 of task acquisition, in which animals traversed a unidirectional 450 cm virtual linear track (for example, Environment 1 (ENV 1)) with a hidden 50 cm reward zone where sucrose water was delivered operantly for licking (Fig. 1d,e). On day 3, the reward zone was moved within a ‘switch’ session to a new track location, signaled by automatic reward delivery on the first ten post-switch trials only if the mouse failed to lick in the new zone. On day 5, the reward zone was moved to a third location, then returned to its original location on day 7. On day 8, the reward switch coincided with the introduction of a novel environment (for example, ENV 2), in which the switch order was then reversed (Fig. 1e). On ‘stay’ days, the last reward location from the previous day was maintained. A separate ‘fixed-condition’ group of mice (n = 3) experienced only one reward location in a single environment (ENV 1) (Extended Data Fig. 1), to control for the effect of experience. To maintain engagement, the reward was randomly omitted on ~15% of trials. At the end of the track, mice passed through a variable length gray ‘teleport’ zone (~50 cm + temporal jitter up to 10 s; Methods) before starting the next lap.\n     4→\n     5→# Behavioral training and VR tasks\n     6→\n     7→## Pre-training\n     8→\n     9→Mice were pre-trained on a ‘running’ task on a 350 cm virtual linear track with random black and white checkerboard walls and a white floor to collect a cued reward. The reward location was marked by two large gray towers, initially positioned 50 cm down the track. The mouse was rewarded for licking within 25 cm of the towers, otherwise, an automatic reward was given at the towers. The towers then disappeared and quickly reappeared further down the track. Covering the distance to the current reward in under 20 s increased the inter-reward distance by 10 cm, but if the mouse took longer than 30 s, the distance decreased by 10 cm, with a maximum reward location of 300 cm. Upon consistent completion of 300 cm distance in under 20 s, the automatic reward was turned off, requiring the mice to lick to receive reward. At the end of the track, the mice passed through a black curtain and into a gray ‘teleport zone’ (50 cm long) that was equiluminant with the VR environment before re-entering the track from the beginning. Once mice were reliably licking and completing 200 laps of the pre-training track within a 30–40 min period, they were advanced to the main task below and imaging (mean ± s.d., 5.8 ± 1.9 days of running pre-training across 14 mice).\n    10→\n    11→## Hidden reward zone task\n    12→\n    13→The main ‘switch’ task involved two virtual environments similar to those previously used to study hippocampal remapping, each with visually distinct features from the pre-training environment. Both environments consisted of a 450 cm linear track, with two colored towers (green in ENV 1, blue in ENV 2) and two patterned towers along the walls. Environments were further distinguished by the frequency of diagonal wall gratings (low, ENV 1; high, ENV 2) and color of the sky (dark gray, ENV 1; light gray, ENV 2). The reward zone was a ‘hidden’, unmarked 50 cm span at one of three possible locations along the track, each equidistantly spaced between the towers to control for proximity of each reward to visual landmarks: zone A, 80–130 cm; zone B, 200–250 cm; zone C, 320–370 cm. Only one reward zone was ever active at a time. Reward was randomly omitted on approximately 15% of trials, determined by a random number generator for each trial. Each lap terminated in a black curtain following by a ‘teleport period’ that began with an unchanging gray display for a randomly jittered amount of time (‘jitter period’) between 1 and 5 s (5–10 s on trials following reward omissions or trials in which the mouse did not lick in the reward zone), after which the gray display began to move again for 50 cm giving the appearance of a gray ‘tunnel,’ before the mouse re-entered the virtual environment at 0 cm.\n    14→\n    15→Each mouse encountered a different starting reward zone and sequence of reward zone switches, counterbalanced across mice (n = 11 mice). Most mice began the task in ENV 1 (n = 9 mice; two mice (m17 and m18) began in ENV 2). An initial reward zone (for example, A as in Fig. 1e) was acquired for days 1–2 of the task. On day 3 (switch one), the zone was moved after 30 trials to one of the two other possible locations on the track (for example, B). On the first ten trials of any new condition, including the first day and each subsequent switch, the reward was automatically delivered at the end of the zone if the mouse had not yet licked within the zone, to signal reward availability. Otherwise, the reward was delivered at the first location within the zone where the mouse licked. After these ten trials, a reward was operantly delivered for licking within the zone, and we observed that the mice often started licking before ten trials elapsed (Extended Data Fig. 1a–c). The new reward zone was maintained on day 4. On day 5 (switch two), the zone was moved to the third possible reward zone (for example, C), maintained on day 6 and moved back to the original location on day 7 (for example, A). On day 8, the reward zone switch coincided with a switch into the novel environment, where the sequence of zone switches was then reversed on the same day-to-day schedule for a total of 14 days (Fig. 1 and Extended Data Fig. 1). Each switch occurred after 30 trials.\n    16→\n    17→An additional ‘fixed-condition’ cohort (n = 3 mice) experienced only ENV 1 and one fixed reward location throughout the 14 days (Extended Data Fig. 1d,f).\n    18→\n    19→We targeted 80–100 trials per session with simultaneous 2P imaging (described below), with one imaging session per day (mean ± s.d., 80.5 ± 7.4 trials across 14 mice, all imaging days). The session was terminated early if the mouse ceased licking or running consistently and/or if the imaging time exceeded 50 min. Before the imaging session, mice were provided 30 ‘warm-up’ trials using the task and reward zone from the previous day to re-acclimate them to the VR setup (using the pre-training environment on day 1 of imaging). Following each daily imaging session, mice were given another ~100 training trials without imaging on the last reward zone seen in the imaging session until they acquired their daily water allotment.\n    20→\n    21→# 2P imaging\n    22→\n    23→We used a resonant-galvo scanning 2P microscope from Neurolabware with Scanbox software (https://scanbox.org, v.4.7) operated through MATLAB (Mathworks) to image the calcium activity of CA1 neurons. Excitation was achieved using 920 nm light from a tunable output femtosecond pulsed laser (Coherent Chameleon Discovery). Laser power modulation was accomplished with a Pockels cell (Conoptics). The typical excitation power, measured at the front face of the objective (Leica ×25, 1.0 NA, 2.6 mm working distance), ranged from 15–68 mW for mice m2–m7, 50–100 mW for m10 and m11, and 13–40 mW for m12–19. The task was imaged starting from day 1 for all mice except m11, for whom imaging started on day 3 owing to lower viral expression. For most animals and sessions, to minimize photodamage and photobleaching, the Pockels cell was used to reduce laser power to minimal levels between trials (during the teleport period), except for mice m11–m14 on task days 1, 7, 8 and 14, and mice m15–m19 on day 1 and all switch days, for which laser power was maintained and imaging continued throughout the teleport period. Photons were collected using Hamamatsu gated GAsP photomultiplier tubes (part H11706-401 for Neurolabware Microscopes). The imaging field of view (FOV) was collected at 1.0 magnification with unidirectional scanning at ~15.5 Hz, resulting in an approximately 0.7 × 0.7 mm FOV (512 × 796 pixels). An electrically tunable lens was used to simultaneously image deep and superficial CA1 in two mice (m17 and m18), in two planes separated by ~27 µm, with frames bidirectionally imaged at ~31 Hz interleaved in the scan for a sampling rate of ~15.5 Hz per plane. In all mice, the same FOV was acquired each session by aligning to a reference image from previous days before the start of data acquisition, with the aid of the ‘searchref’ plugin in Scanbox. This allowed us to track single cells across days.\n    24→\n    25→# Calcium data processing\n    26→\n    27→The Suite2P software package (v.0.10.3) was used to perform xy motion correction (rigid and non-rigid) and identify putative cell regions of interest (ROIs). Manual curation eliminated ROIs containing multiple somata or dendrites, lacking visually obvious transients, suspected of overexpressing the calcium indicator or exhibiting high and continuous fluorescence fluctuation typical of putative interneurons. This approach yielded 155–2172 putative pyramidal neurons per session, owing to variation in imaging window implant quality and viral expression. In multi-plane imaged animals (see ‘2P imaging’), ROIs were identified separately per plane, but planes were pooled for all analyses except those in Extended Data Fig. 7. Custom code was used to follow individual ROIs across imaging sessions using the intersection over union of their pixels. The threshold for ROI matching was chosen algorithmically for each dataset such that the intersection over union for the best match for an ROI pair was always greater than the intersection over union for any second-best match.\n    28→\n    29→To compute the ΔF/F (dF/F) for each ROI, baseline fluorescence was calculated within each trial independently using a maximin procedure with a 20 s sliding window. Limitation to individual trials both accounts for potential photobleaching over the session and avoids the teleport periods for sessions during which the laser power was reduced (see ‘2P imaging’). The value of dF/F was then calculated for each cell as the fluorescence minus the baseline, divided by the absolute value of the baseline, then smoothed with a two-sample (~0.129 s) s.d. Gaussian kernel. The activity rate was extracted by deconvolving dF/F with a canonical calcium kernel using the OASIS algorithm as used in Suite2p. We note that this deconvolution is not interpreted as a spike rate but rather as a method to eliminate the asymmetric smoothing of the calcium signal induced by indicator kinetics. Additional putative interneurons were detected for exclusion from further analysis by a Pearson correlation of >0.5 between their dF/F timeseries and the animal’s running speed, excluding 0.42 ± 0.85% of cells (mean ± s.d. across mice and days).\n    30→\n    31→# Statistics and reproducibility\n    32→\n    33→To avoid assumptions about data distributions, we used nonparametric tests or permutation tests in most cases, except when a Shapiro–Wilk test for normality confirmed that parametric tests were reasonable. In the main text, percentages of cells classified by remapping type are reported as mean ± s.d. out of all place cells identified on the specified days. Cells were excluded from certain analyses only if they did not meet pre-established criteria as ‘place cells’ (defined in ‘Place cell identification’), to ensure that included cells provided
